The added responsibility initially seemed to energize her; she reorganized her sister’s home, took on extra consulting work, and launched a small online shop for her pottery late at night, telling friends she finally felt \"in her flow.\" Over time, the travel, disrupted sleep, and constant juggling between caregiving and work led to a marked slump—she started missing bill payments more often, canceling art fairs, and feeling paralyzed when trying to plan even simple store-layout projects. After about two months of this decline, a longtime client gently pointed out that her work had become uncharacteristically scattered, and combined with her own worry about her fading mental sharpness, she agreed to be evaluated by a psychiatrist referred through her primary care clinic.\n- Chief Complaint: My mind feels foggy and I can’t seem to focus or make decisions the way I used to.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S024 (Depressed_Mood): Most days lately I wake up with this heavy, sinking feeling in my chest, like someone flipped a switch and drained all the color out \n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): Lately my brain feels like it’s full of fog, so that even choosing which display to put near the entrance of a store or deciding what route to take to my sister’s easier for everyone if I just didn’t wake up one morning, and while I haven’t made any plans to hurt myself, those kinds of thoughts have been popping up more often and lingering. \n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): Back when my sister first got sick and I threw myself into rearranging her house and taking on extra clients, I felt unusually upbeat and wired, like I was on a roll and her closets, drafting new proposals for clients, and tinkering with my pottery website because I felt like I had endless energy and couldn’t bear the idea of just sitting still. \n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): I remember telling my friends, only half joking, that I was the only one who could really get my sister’s life and my business in order because I was so efficient and creative, like I had some special knack other people just didn’t have. \n- S039 (Manic_Distractibility): When I was in that more revved‑up phase, I couldn’t stay focused on one thing—I'd be working on a merchandising plan and then a text ping, a bird outside the window, or a new idea about reorganizing my sister’s pantry would pull me off track, and I’d jump from one task to another without finishing. \n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): I packed my days with back‑to‑back client meetings, constant phone calls checking on my sister, and late‑night pottery and website updates, piling on more projects than I could reasonably handle because it felt like I had to keep doing and fixing everything at once.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last couple of months I’ve had to turn down new consulting projects, postpone work for longtime clients, and lean on my husband to handle things like paying bills and keeping the house in order because I just After about two months of this decline, a longtime client gently pointed out that her work had become uncharacteristically scattered, and combined with her own worry about her fading mental sharpness, finishing. \n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): I packed my days with back‑to‑back client meetings, constant phone calls checking on my sister, and late‑night pottery and website updates, piling on Lately, my mind has been feeling very foggy, and I just can’t seem to focus or make decisions the way I used to. It’s gotten to the point where it's really affecting my day-to-day life, so I decided it was time to get some help.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of fogginess and difficulty focusing before in your life, or is this the first time you have felt this way?\n\nPatient: Looking back, these big swings from feeling overly revved up to months of feeling foggy and drained have actually been coming and going for several years. However, this current low stretch really took hold after my sister’s heart problems worsened.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt 'overly revved up,' did you find that you needed much less sleep than usual, felt unusually energetic, or found yourself taking on many new projects or risks?\n\nPatient: Yes, during those times I felt incredibly wired and energetic, often staying up half the night reorganizing my sister's closets or updating my website without feeling tired at all. I would pile on way too many client projects and tasks all at once because I felt completely unstoppable and couldn't sit still.\n\nDoctor: During those 'revved up' periods, did your behavior become so extreme that it caused significant problems at work or in your relationships, or did you require hospitalization to keep you safe?\n\nPatient: No, it never got to the point of needing hospitalization, and it didn't cause major problems at the time—I actually felt highly productive, energized, and completely in my flow, though I did occasionally snap at people if they slowed me down. The real problems, like missing bill payments and having to turn down work, only started when I crashed into this current foggy, low phase.\n\nDoctor: During your current low phase, have you experienced a persistent loss of interest or pleasure in almost all of your usual activities, or do you find you can still enjoy things if the circumstances are right?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've completely lost interest in the things I used to love. I used to get so much joy out of sketching store layouts or throwing pottery for my shop, but lately, I look at the clay and feel absolutely nothing. I usually just end up turning off the lights and going back to bed or watching TV.\n\nDoctor: During these low periods, have you ever felt that life is not worth living or had any thoughts of harming yourself?"
  }

Yes, on the drives back from my sister's, I sometimes catch myself thinking it might be easier for everyone if I just didn't wake up one morning. I haven't made any plans to hurt myself, but those kinds of thoughts have been popping up and lingering more often lately.

However, this current low stretch really took hold after my sister’s heart problems worsened.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt 'overly revved up,' did you find that you needed much less sleep than usual, felt unusually energetic, or found yourself taking on many new projects or risks?\n\nPatient: Yes, during those times I felt incredibly wired and energetic, often staying up half the night reorganizing my sister's closets or updating my website without feeling tired at all. I would pile on way too many client projects and tasks all at once because I felt completely unstoppable and couldn't sit still.\n\nDoctor: During those 'revved up' periods, did your behavior become so extreme that it caused significant problems at work or in your relationships, or did you require hospitalization to keep you safe?\n\nPatient: No, it never got to the point of needing hospitalization, and it didn't cause major problems at the time—I actually felt highly productive, energized, and completely in my flow, though I did occasionally snap at people if they slowed me down. The real problems, like missing bill payments and having to turn down work, only started when I crashed into this current foggy, low phase.\n\nDoctor: During your current low phase, have you experienced a persistent loss of interest or pleasure in almost all of your usual activities, or do you find you can still enjoy things if the circumstances are right?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've completely lost interest in the things I used to love. I used to get so much joy out of sketching store layouts or throwing pottery for my shop, but lately, I look at the clay and feel absolutely nothing. I usually just end up turning off the lights and going back to bed or watching TV.\n\nDoctor: During these low periods, have you ever felt that life is not worth living or had any thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, on the drives back from my sister's, I sometimes catch myself thinking it might be easier for everyone if I just didn't wake up one morning. I haven't made any plans to hurt myself, but those kinds of thoughts have been popping up and lingering more often lately.\n\nDoctor: During this current period, have you experienced any physical slowing, significant changes in your appetite or weight, or a sense of worthlessness that makes it difficult for you to function for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel physically slowed down, like I'm moving through molasses just walking to my sister's door, and I'll sit staring at my computer for ages before I can type. I also constantly beat myself up with guilt, feeling worthless and like I'm letting everyone down, though I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite or weight."

I would pile on way too many client projects and tasks all at once because I felt completely unstoppable and couldn't sit still.\n\nDoctor: During those 'revved up' periods, did your behavior become so extreme that it caused significant problems at work or in your relationships, or did you require hospitalization to keep you safe?\n\nPatient: No, it never got to the point of needing hospitalization, and it didn't cause major problems at the time—I actually felt highly productive, energized, and completely in my flow, though I did occasionally snap at people if they slowed me down. I haven't made any plans to hurt myself, but those kinds of thoughts have been popping up and lingering more often lately.\n\nDoctor: During this current period, have you experienced any physical slowing, significant changes in your appetite or weight, or a sense of worthlessness that makes it difficult for you to function for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel physically slowed down, like I'm moving through molasses just walking to my sister's door, and I'll sit staring at my computer for ages before I can type. I also constantly beat myself up with guilt, feeling worthless and like I'm letting everyone down, though I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite or weight.\n\nDoctor: During this current period, have these feelings of worthlessness and lack of energy been so severe that you have been unable to perform your work or household tasks for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have severely impacted my daily life. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
